| Opaque token framing for all six purposes | `CovenantEnvelopeCodec` over `CovenantEnvelopeMasterKeyProvider` |
| Live-process key and snapshot transitions | `CovenantAuthorityTransitionPublisher`, under a still-held exclusive lease |
| Startup authority publication and key take | `CovenantAuthorityStartupReconciler`, after commit and before readiness |
| Campaign truth table | `CanonicalCampaignResolutionPolicy` (pure) |
| Physical root identity | `PhysicalCampaignRootOpener` and `CampaignRootIdentityKeyProvider` |
| Registered-root and availability lookup | `CampaignPathIdentityReader`, `CampaignAvailabilityReader` |
| Session creation and assistant begin | `ISessionTurnBeginStore` on `GrimoireRepository` |

**Deferred to later slices.** Covenant content loading, prompt placement, token attribution, the turn plan, the staging collector, and the batch-aware finalizer land in issue #84 and are described in §10.13. MCP proposal and retirement tools, the `X-Arcanum-Context-Policy` pre-binding middleware, and the operator API and CLI surfaces belong to issues #85 and later. `ArcanumInvocationContexts.NoContextRequestFeatureKey` is read today and set by nothing, so every route takes its typed default until that middleware lands.

### 10.13 Covenant prompt planning and atomic turn publication

Issue #84 implements the runtime slice that turns a linked Covenant plan into prompt bytes and turns a completed response into durable state. It adds deterministic prompt placement, typed token attribution, per-attempt admission, the disclosure journal, the per-turn staging collector, and the one batch-aware assistant finalizer. The MCP proposal and retirement tools, the operator API and CLI surfaces, and the `Arcanum:Features:Covenant` configuration key remain with issues #85 and later; the runtime reads the published `CovenantAvailabilitySnapshot.FeatureEnabled` flag, so a host that never enables the capability adds no Covenant prompt bytes and performs no Covenant read.

**Placement is decided once and never re-derived.** `SystemPromptBuilder` takes an optional `CovenantPromptContent` and owns both headings, the untrusted-data notice, and nothing else; every byte between the Proposed heading and its closing fence came from the compiler, which already chose a fence longer than any backtick run it contains. Confirmed content renders in `CONTEXT` after Workspace and before Codex, Global before Campaign, and only a section with content emits a heading. Proposed content renders in `DATA` before Lexicon as one fenced block. Covenant framing uses explicit `\n` rather than the platform separator, because those exact bytes are hashed into the admission receipt and the provider-call envelope and the same admitted content must not produce two section digests on two machines.

**Attribution replaces heading parsing.** `SystemPromptDocument.BuildResult()` renders once and produces a `SystemPromptAttributionMap`: an ordered, nonoverlapping partition of the whole prompt into `Preamble`, `DataHeader`, `CovenantProposed`, `DataBody`, `WorkspaceContext`, `CovenantConfirmed`, `ContextBody`, and `Instructions`. `ModelTokenEstimator` charges the two Covenant lanes from that partition and runs its existing heading-and-fence classifier over the prompt with the Covenant spans removed, so every pre-existing source keeps its previous count and an untrusted line that imitates `### The Covenant, Global Confirmed` moves no tokens at all. `ContextTokenSource` gains `CovenantConfirmed` and `CovenantProposed` so `mana` and `context inspect` attribute the profile separately. `SystemPromptTokenAttribution` performs the exact one-pass variant with `EncodeToTokens` whenever the tokenizer reports usable UTF-16 offsets, assigns each token to the span containing its first covered code unit, charges zero-length and uncovered offsets to `SpecialOrUncovered`, and reports honestly when it had to fall back to per-span counting.

**One plan per turn, one admission per attempt.** `ICovenantContextProvider` answers eligibility from `ArcanumInvocationContext` first, then the availability snapshot, then Campaign resolution, and only then takes a turn lease and opens the bounded canonical read. That order is the contract: even the shape of a later failure is an answer about Covenant state, so an ineligible caller must be refused before anything is observed on its behalf. The resulting `CovenantTurnContext` holds the immutable plan, the lease, and the staging collector for the whole logical turn, and every absence carries a typed reason rather than a null plan and a log line. `CovenantAdmissionPlanner` then derives each attempt's outcome from that reused plan: Confirmed is all-or-fail, Proposed keeps the longest prefix whose complete framed section fits, removals happen in reverse plan order with the exact remaining section retokenized after each one, and the loop is bounded at 32 removals. Two fallback candidates with different tokenizers and different remaining budgets therefore reach different Proposed admissions from the same bytes and the same revision vector.

**Acknowledgement precedes disclosure.** `ICovenantDisclosureJournal` commits a durable receipt before bytes leave Arcanum, never after. A journal written afterwards cannot record the one case it exists for — a dispatch that left the process and then crashed — and the operator would be told nothing was disclosed while the provider already held the payload. Ordinal allocation, the subject counters, the rolling chain head, and the receipt row all move inside one immediate transaction, because an ordinal allocated outside it is an ordinal two parallel attempts can both claim. The journal is accounting and never content: it records a destination class, not a destination.

**Staging is provisional; publication is atomic.** `CovenantMutationCollector` accepts typed intents for one eligible turn and writes nothing. It resolves exact tool replay before target uniqueness and before capacity, because the reverse order turns a retry of an already-staged call into a duplicate-target rejection; it partitions intents by provider branch and carries the shared prefix into a replacement branch while discarding the abandoned suffix; it refuses to resume an abandoned branch at all; and it seals only when no tool call is still in flight. `IGrimoireTurnCommitter`, implemented on `GrimoireRepository`, is then the single writer of every finalization. Assistant content, the one-shot `assistant_entry_finalizations` guard, its consumed capacity reservation, and any sealed Covenant batch commit inside one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction on the connection EF already owns. The guard row, not the content, is what proves a turn finished: a successful empty response is an ordinary committed guard, and a retry resolves through the stored outcome instead of running a second turn. A publication arm that cannot run rolls back the response with it.

**Two latent defects this slice had to fix.** EF stores a `Guid` as an uppercase `D`-format TEXT literal, while the capacity ledger bound lowercase, so every reservation and guard would have failed its foreign key to `Sessions` in a real host while passing against suites that seeded their own lowercase rows; identities in those core tables are now bound as `Guid` values so the provider produces exactly the text EF stored. And the `installation_turn_quota_state` singleton the schema comment promises was seeded by nothing, so the counter move behind every finalization reported a missing row; `CoreGrimoireSchemaDataInitializer` now seeds the exactly zeroed singleton, which is the one shape the table's insert trigger accepts without a turn-capacity authorization.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| Prompt placement, cache descriptors, attribution partition | `SystemPromptBuilder` → `SystemPromptBuildResult` |
| Typed token attribution | `SystemPromptAttributionMap`, `SystemPromptTokenAttribution`, `ModelTokenEstimator` |
| One plan and one lease per logical turn | `ICovenantContextProvider` → `CovenantTurnContext` |
| Per-attempt admission and pressure | `CovenantAdmissionPlanner` → `CovenantAdmissionReceipt` |
| Disclosure evidence before dispatch | `ICovenantDisclosureJournal` |
| Provisional staging and branch lineage | `ICovenantMutationCollector` |
| Atomic response and mutation publication | `IGrimoireTurnCommitter` on `GrimoireRepository` |

**Deferred to later slices.** The MCP `propose_covenant` and `retire_covenant` tools and their sensitive-argument transport land in issue #85 and are described in §10.14; the public contract those surfaces are built from is frozen in issue #88 and described in §10.16, while the operator API, CLI, and the `Arcanum:Features:Covenant` key belong to issue #89; durable public turn claims with lease adoption, maintenance-step checkpointing, and prior-boot recovery belong to the recovery slices. The components in this section are composed and registered, and the live provider loop adopts them as those surfaces land.

### 10.14 Covenant MCP mutation and sensitive-egress controls

Issue #85 adds the two model-facing Covenant mutation tools and the machinery that makes them safe to advertise: a private streaming transport buffer, a single-use capability bridge, a dynamic Ward policy for sensitive egress, and an acknowledgement that commits before any physical effect. Nothing here writes canonical state — both tools stage into the §10.13 collector, and the batch-aware assistant finalizer stays the only writer.

**A tool call has no identity until it is complete.** `ProviderToolCallBuffer` holds every streamed tool-name and argument fragment by call index and exposes exactly one exit, `Freeze`. Classifying a prefix is the failure this prevents: a call whose name is still `propose_` is indistinguishable from an ordinary tool for precisely as long as it takes to publish its arguments to SSE, progress, transcript, or diagnostics. Arguments may arrive before, between, and after the name they belong to, several call indexes interleave with isolated buffers, and a multibyte UTF-8 sequence may straddle two fragments while a malformed one is refused as early as it can be proven wrong. The code-owned bounds are 64 simultaneous call indexes, 256 name bytes, 65,536 argument bytes per call, and 262,144 aggregate buffered bytes per attempt; every counter is charged with the raw delta *before* the buffer grows, and crossing any bound aborts the whole attempt, clears every private buffer, and returns `Hub.ProviderToolBufferExceeded` before classification or dispatch. A changed final name or call id, a reused index, and a malformed or incomplete termination each return `Hub.ProviderToolCallInvalid` and retire that index for the life of the attempt.

**Classification runs once, over the frozen call.** `CovenantToolClassifier` produces the risk identity, whether the arguments are private, and the two evidence digests a Ward receipt and a disclosure receipt bind: SHA-256 over the exact UTF-8 name and over the RFC 8785 canonical arguments. Canonicalization is what stops one request from producing two evidence digests, and a body that cannot be canonicalized has no stable identity to consent to, so it fails rather than proceeding. `ProviderToolCallClassification.ToolInputDigest` binds the name and the canonical arguments and deliberately nothing else — including a capability nonce or an attempt ordinal would turn an honest retry into an idempotency conflict.

**One capability, one call.** `CovenantToolInvocationContext` carries the turn's collector, its canonical Campaign, the admission receipt that produced this call, the call-scoped materialization snapshot, the bounded head probe, and — for a retirement — the resolved preflight and the Ward receipt. The in-process MCP server runs on its own task, so the turn's async flow never reaches a handler; `CovenantToolCapabilityRegistry` bridges that gap on the exact connection-and-request id plus a random 128-bit nonce. Every rule in that table exists because JSON-RPC request ids repeat over a long-lived connection: registration is `TryAdd` only, taking is one-shot, the id stays reserved until the handler removes it, removal compares the key *and* the exact registration so an earlier request's late cleanup cannot free the id a later one owns, and the TTL sweep reclaims only registrations nobody ever took. States are `Registered → Taken → Closing → Disposed`; `Closing` exists so a use that suspended across an `await` has something to fail against, and disposal drains outstanding leases rather than setting a flag. `RecheckBeforeIrreversibleEffect` revalidates state, nonce, cancellation, collector lifecycle, and branch admission immediately before any staging action, because between handler entry and staging the turn can be cancelled, the collector can seal, and a provider fallback can abandon the branch this call was admitted on.

**Sensitive egress is attended, and disabling Wards does not make it unattended.** `CovenantEgressWardPolicy` resolves against the *live* `ArcanumInvocationContext` rather than a value captured when the turn began. An ineligible invocation is denied before a Ward is placed; a proposal is `SensitivePayloadOnly` — private arguments, no Ward, because the Proposed lane is review-only and cannot change Confirmed content; a retirement requires attended approval. Configured auto-approval applies only when the operator both enabled the policy and named `retire_covenant`. One rule diverges from `ToolRiskClassifier.RequiresWard` on purpose: with Wards switched off an ordinary Forbidden Art executes unwarded, but Covenant retirement is denied, because switching Wards off removes the operator's only chance to refuse and silence is not consent to erase their own standing instructions. `retire_covenant` is an *intrinsic* Ward tool for the same reason — replacing the configurable forbidden-arts list is a statement about which tools the operator considers risky, not a waiver of the prompt for that one.

**Acknowledgement precedes the effect, and the guard owns both halves.** `CovenantToolEgressGuard.DiscloseThenAsync` commits an `McpToolUse` receipt through `ICovenantDisclosureJournal` and only then runs the effect; a journal that cannot commit stops the effect entirely. The ordering lives inside one method rather than in an "acknowledge" call a caller could forget. `CovenantToolEgressAttempt.PhysicalAttemptOrdinal` is what keeps the accounting honest: a retry after a timeout and a reconnect after a dropped stream are separate physical disclosures even with identical arguments, so each advances the ordinal and is counted separately, while a genuine replay of the same attempt keeps its ordinal and reuses the acknowledged identity. The journal is allowed to overstate and never to understate.

**Both tools are registered inert and advertised live.** `ArcanumInternalToolServer` always registers both handlers in its cached internal-tool superset, so runtime enablement and schema-repair recovery work without rebuilding `McpConnectionManager` partitions. Advertisement is filtered per turn from the feature gate and canonical availability, and each handler rechecks the same facts, so a stale cached partition or a direct internal invocation still fails closed with `Covenant.IneligibleTurn` or `Covenant.Unavailable`. Neither name can be shadowed by an external MCP server, and both are blocked by name from the diagnostic invocation endpoint.

**The schemas are audited on what they omit.** `propose_covenant` accepts a key and authored content; `retire_covenant` accepts a key and a lane. Scope, Campaign id, origin, lifecycle, revision, attachment ids, and every authority field are absent, so agent-originated Global mutation is unrepresentable on the wire as well as refused by the server. Proposal provenance is the exact `ProviderCallMaterializationSnapshot` of the call that emitted the request — never the turn-cumulative attachment gate, which would let content materialized for an earlier call authorize a later proposal that never saw it — and staging fails closed on an unprovenanced snapshot or more than 64 sources. The expected Proposed revision comes from the turn plan when the key is materialized there and otherwise from one bounded indexed head probe; revision zero is proved, never assumed, and a tombstone refuses agent reactivation instead of silently resurrecting a key the operator retired. The turn snapshot projects no key-reclamation epoch, so a plan hit expects the unreclaimed epoch zero — conservatively, not silently: publication reads the real epoch inside its write transaction and refuses the whole batch with `Covenant.StaleSnapshot` if it disagrees, so only a key that was reclaimed and then re-materialized into the same plan is affected, and it fails closed. A retirement stages only the target its preflight resolved, so a call that now names a different key or lane is refused as not the call the operator approved.

**What the model gets back says less than the operator can see.** Both tools return a compact text fallback plus typed `structuredContent` (`McpToolDefinitionWire.OutputSchema` and `McpToolsCallResultWire.StructuredContent` are new optional MCP wire fields, source-generated like the rest). The staged receipt carries the mutation id, an opaque target digest, scope, lane, operation, expected revision, rendered hash, and byte cost — no key, no authored content, no authority field. Its status is `staged`, never `published`: the change is written only if the turn's own answer commits. An expected failure is structured too, so the model can tell "the operator declined" from "this key already has a staged change" without parsing prose, and its message is code-owned and never echoes what it failed on.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| Private streaming tool-call fragments | `ProviderToolCallBuffer` → `FrozenProviderToolCall` |
| Frozen-call classification and evidence digests | `CovenantToolClassifier` → `ProviderToolCallClassification` |
| Single-use per-call capability | `CovenantToolInvocationContext` |
| Connection-and-request-id capability bridge | `CovenantToolCapabilityRegistry` |
| Attended sensitive-egress policy and Ward evidence | `CovenantEgressWardPolicy` → `CovenantToolWardReceipt` |
| Acknowledgement before a physical effect | `CovenantToolEgressGuard` |
| Agent-authored intent construction | `CovenantAgentMutationFactory` |
| Tool schemas, gating, and staging handlers | `ArcanumInternalToolServer` Covenant partials |

**Deferred to later slices.** The API pipeline that mints a capability per provider-emitted tool call, resolves the retirement preflight outside the inference hot path, raises the Ward, and drives the egress guard lands with the operator surfaces; until then the components are composed and registered and no turn produces a capability, so both tools stay unadvertised and every direct call is refused.

### 10.15 Covenant derived-output protection and host-process taint

Issue #86 answers two questions the earlier slices left open. The first is what happens to content *derived* from Covenant material once it leaves the prompt: an assistant answer, a summary of that answer, a title generated from it, a tool artifact it produced. The second is what happens to Covenant itself when an installation enables the unsandboxed host-process escape hatch, which hands model-driven code the operator's own operating-system identity.

**The label is written by one type, inside the caller's transaction.** `artifact_sensitivity` has existed since issue #81 with append-only guard triggers; `ArtifactSensitivityLedger` is the writer those triggers were waiting for. Every derived-output producer routes through `WriteWithinAsync`, which takes the connection and transaction the caller already owns and neither commits nor rolls back. That is what makes "the artifact and its label share a fate" a property rather than an intention: a Covenant-derived response whose label did not land would be protected content sitting in the Grimoire with nothing recording that it is protected, and every reader downstream — cache filters, exports, generic search — would treat it as ordinary. `IGrimoireTurnCommitter` therefore labels the assistant entry in the same immediate transaction as the response and the one-shot guard, and a label failure fails the whole finalization.

**Nothing can be relabelled, and nothing can be relabelled clean.** The rows are immutable by trigger, so the ledger never issues an update. It still checks for an existing label before writing one, because reaching the trigger would abort the caller's transaction with a raw SQL error instead of a typed refusal. Repeating the byte-identical label is an idempotent replay; anything else — a second label for the same artifact identity, or a clean relabel of a tainted one — is `Covenant.IntegrityFailure`. A discarded placeholder is deliberately not labelled: there is no durable artifact left to describe, and a label pointing at a deleted entry would keep the Session projection tainted for content nobody can read.

**`session_sensitivity_state` is maintained in the same transaction and is order-insensitive.** The response-cache filter reads exactly this row once per request, before any cache probe, so it must never be something recomputed from labels on a hot path. Its provenance digest is recomputed from the *distinct* provenance values of the Session's live labels rather than chained onto the previous digest: a chain would be order-sensitive, so two Sessions holding the same generations would fingerprint differently depending on the order their artifacts happened to be written, and the value would stop being comparable — which is the only thing a projection digest is for. Distinct is what keeps it bounded, because a dataset generation changes on reset and restore, not per turn.

**The two mutable Session columns get immutable artifacts.** `Sessions.Summary` and `Sessions.Title` are the two places model output overwrites itself in place, and issue #74 cannot `ALTER TABLE` either. `SessionDerivedArtifactStore` implements both through one replacement protocol: insert the next revision's artifact, move the composite-keyed current pointer, write the column, label the new revision, and only then delete the prior artifact and its label under the `ArtifactReplacement` authorization. Deleting first would leave a window in which the pointer's foreign key had nothing to name; labelling last means a label failure aborts before any evidence is destroyed. A model-generated title propagates taint, and a clean operator-authored replacement is the one path that removes a prior tainted label — in the transaction that overwrites the bytes it described.

**A protected read is one snapshot and one lease.** `ProtectedAssistantArtifactReader` reads the entry and its label inside a single deferred transaction, because two reads would let a label be written between them and the interesting window is exactly the one a tainted response is finalized in. It verifies that the label's content digest describes the bytes actually present and that its Session owner matches, and it fails closed on either mismatch rather than downgrading to an unlabelled read. The lease is the gate's existing generation-bound `ICovenantSnapshotReadLease`, revalidated on entry and again before the value is handed back, so a reset or erasure that lands mid-read cannot be beaten by a fast query. An artifact belonging to another Session is `Covenant.NotFound` rather than a distinct refusal, because a distinct code would confirm the identity exists somewhere in the installation.

**Logs, metrics, traces, and progress get a type they cannot smuggle content through.** `CovenantProtectedLogScope` carries a sensitivity, a provenance mode, a generation *count*, an opaque diagnostic tag, and the owning Session and Campaign — and nothing else. Reducing a real `ArtifactSensitivityLabel` to one drops the content digest, the sensitivity digest, the label digest, and the producing plan and admission digests, all of which are correlatable evidence about protected content even though none is readable. Its rendering is a fixed vocabulary with no free text, so a structured sink and a plain-text sink disclose the same nothing. Guidance to "avoid logging Covenant content" is advice a later change silently stops following; a value that cannot carry content is a guarantee that survives whoever writes the next log line.

**The downstream inventory is closed and enforced in both directions.** `CovenantDerivedOutputInventory` declares every producer and consumer with one of four policies — `PropagateLabel`, `RefuseTainted`, `ContentFreeMetadataOnly`, `ProtectedReadRequired` — plus the owning type and a stated reason. `CovenantDerivedOutputInventoryTests` walks the shipping assemblies for implementations of the governed contracts and fails when one has no declared policy, and walks the inventory and fails when an entry names a type that no longer exists. Either direction alone would rot: an inventory nobody adds to, or an inventory full of guarantees nothing provides. Four policies rather than a boolean, because "handles taint" is not one behaviour, and collapsing them would make the safest of them the only one anybody implemented — or the loosest of them the default.

**The escape hatch is an installation transition, not a setting.** Enabling unsandboxed host-process tools cannot coexist with Covenant authority, so `HostProcessToolsTransitionService` performs an offline, permanently tainting transition rather than flipping a flag. It requires Development edition, the exact `ARCANUM_ALLOW_HOST_PROCESS_TOOLS` opt-in, a stopped host proven by the installation lock, zero Covenant canonical rows and protected artifacts, and a process that has never opened Covenant — the last of which `CovenantProcessResidence` latches at the one canonical connection choke point and at envelope-key derivation, because a process holding decrypted pages cannot be the one that authorizes arbitrary same-identity code.

**Two markers, because either alone is forgeable by an ordinary operation.** A database restore carries an archived authority row into a clean installation; an OS secret survives a database reset. The transition commits `PendingHostToolsTaint` to the core row *before* touching the dedicated `host-process-tools-taint` credential slot, so an unattributable marker can never exist, then reads the slot back, joins the pair, and only then compare-and-swaps to `HostToolsTainted` while advancing the authority and recovery-envelope epochs. Compensation is available exactly once: when the slot *proves* it refused the write and a readback proves it empty, the call that created the pending row may return it to clean. Every other outcome — a faulted write, an unreadable slot, a lost compare-and-swap — leaves the installation pending and blocked, because a clean answer after a possible escape is the one answer that can never be corrected. Retrying the same transition identity resumes by phase; a different identity is refused rather than allowed to adopt a transition it did not start.

A platform with no credential backend at all is treated as an absent slot rather than an unreadable one, because a backend that does not exist cannot be holding a marker and a write into it is refused rather than uncertain. The consequence is deliberate on both sides: a headless host with no secret service still starts normally, and it can never enable the escape hatch, because the transition has nowhere to record that it happened. A genuinely tainted installation is unaffected — its database row still says so, and a tainted row beside an absent marker is a mismatch.

**The join is pure, and only one of its four answers grants anything.** `HostProcessToolsMarkerPairJoiner` compares installation identity, transition identity, taint-time master-key version, fingerprint, and the derived taint-identity digest without short-circuiting — not because the fingerprint is secret, but so an early return cannot leak *which* field disagreed to a caller that can retry with a crafted marker. Clean plus absent is `Clean`; pending is `PendingBlocked` with or without its matching marker; tainted plus an exactly matching marker is `TaintedMatched`; everything else is `MismatchBlocked`.

**The startup gate runs before anything can hold Covenant bytes.** `HostProcessToolsStartupGate` is the first phase permitted to classify the installation, on the install connection rather than a pool. It reads the operating-system marker *first* and blocks before opening the database at all when that marker is unreadable or malformed, because the database is the marker a restore can replace and a process must never form an opinion from it while the independent one is unknown. A clean pair started with the escape-hatch environment blocks with `Covenant.HostToolsTransitionRequired` and the exact offline command, rather than honouring an opt-in that would enable arbitrary host execution without recording that it happened. A matching tainted pair publishes a permanent no-Covenant `HostProcessToolsRuntimePolicy`, which `CovenantAuthorityStartupReconciler` consults before publishing any authority snapshot or deriving any envelope key; removing the opt-in afterwards suppresses the tool and restores nothing, and the policy refuses to be republished as Covenant-permitting inside a process that has already been told otherwise.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| The one label writer, inside the caller's transaction | `ArtifactSensitivityLedger` |
| Required sensitivity on every derived write | `DerivedArtifactWrite` → `IArtifactSensitivityLedger` |
| Content digest a label binds to | `DerivedArtifactContentDigest` |
| Immutable summary and title artifacts | `SessionDerivedArtifactStore` |
| Same-snapshot protected read under a lease | `ProtectedAssistantArtifactReader` |
| Content-free logging, metrics, and progress | `CovenantProtectedLogScope` |
| Closed producer and consumer inventory | `CovenantDerivedOutputInventory` |
| Offline, permanently tainting transition | `HostProcessToolsTransitionService` |
| Pure database-and-OS marker classification | `HostProcessToolsMarkerPairJoiner` |
| First-phase startup classification and policy | `HostProcessToolsStartupGate` → `HostProcessToolsRuntimePolicy` |

**One block is an instruction rather than a stop, and only until #89.** A host started with the escape hatch armed against a provably clean installation is the exact situation the offline enable command exists to resolve — and that command ships with the operator surfaces. Hard-failing today would leave a Development host unable to start *and* unable to complete the transition, so the gate still classifies and publishes the block, `HostProcessToolsStartupBlocker.EscapeHatchWithoutTransition` distinguishes it, and the bootstrap logs the remediation and continues with Covenant unpermitted for the life of the process. Every other blocked disposition means the durable evidence disagrees with itself, which cannot happen without a transition having been attempted, and those still stop startup.

**Deferred to later slices.** The `arcanum security host-process-tools enable --yes` command that drives the transition port, the conditional-read HTTP metadata and no-store headers on every derived route, plaintext export refusal and Campaign-export exclusion counts, generic-search partition separation, the request-bound maintenance authority that feeds Loremaster, Saga, and Lexicon their frozen tainted backlog, and reset and erasure over labelled sinks all land with the public and lifecycle slices (#87 through #94). The components here are composed and registered; the ledger is reached today only by the assistant finalizer and the two Session artifact stores.

### 10.16 Covenant public and recovery contract freeze

Issue #88 ships no endpoint and no command. It ships the shapes every remaining Covenant surface will be built out of, and the inventories that stop a second version of any of them from appearing. That ordering is deliberate: #87, #89, #90, and #94 are four slices that each need the same DTOs, the same error codes, the same ports, and the same durable identities, and four slices inventing them independently is how a codebase ends up with `CovenantEntryDto` beside `CovenantHeadDto` — both correct, subtly different, and impossible to remove once a client has seen either.

**A request shape validates itself.** Every public Covenant request record owns a `Validate()` that returns the exact typed refusal, and `CovenantWireValidation` owns the rules those methods share. The alternative — validation inside each endpoint — makes the rule a property of whichever surface got there first, and the CLI, the Compendium, and a later caller each get to be wrong independently. The specific defect this closes is the omitted scope: `CovenantCursorScopeSelection` has no zero member, an absent JSON property deserializes to zero, and a zero that reached the store would be an installation-wide read nobody asked for. Every request states its scope explicitly or is refused, which is what "never searches every Campaign by accidental omission" means in code rather than in prose.

**Bounds are measured in the unit the storage layer counts.** Search text is capped at 512 UTF-8 bytes and 32 terms; authored content at 2,048 UTF-8 bytes; a cursor or token at the envelope's 4,096-character encoded bound, checked before anything tries to decode it. UTF-8 rather than characters, because 256 astral characters are 1,024 bytes and a character-counting bound would admit four times the declared cost. Page size is the one field that is clamped rather than refused — a caller asking for a thousand rows wants a page, not an error — and `EffectiveLimit` reports what it actually got.

**Two ambiguities are unrepresentable rather than refused.** A detail lookup carries `CovenantScope`, which has no all-scopes member, because the same key can exist in Global and in every Campaign and "the" entry would be a guess. A `Set` carries no lane, because Confirmed is the only lane an operator authors, and a lane field would make "operator writes Proposed" a validation rule instead of an impossibility. Global Proposed retirement is the one pairing that is checked rather than typed away, since retirement legitimately names a lane.

**One port per surface, and the ports say so out loud.** `ICovenantManagementService`, `ICovenantMutationService`, `ICovenantMaintenanceService`, `ICampaignPathIdentityService`, and `ISessionCampaignBindingService` are the five seams #87 and #89 implement. Management is one port rather than one per route because the routes share a lease, a snapshot policy, and an authority requirement, and splitting them would let a later surface acquire coverage the others deliberately do not have. Prepare and apply live on one port because the shared preflight calculator is what makes the token binding meaningful; a second port that could prepare without the one that commits would be a second opinion about the same effect.

**The inventory is enforced in both directions and admits its exceptions.** `CovenantPublicContractInventory` declares every wire shape, every port, and every durable checkpoint, and the architecture suite fails when a declared entry names a type that no longer exists *and* when a public `*Request`/`*Dto` in the Covenant namespace is not declared. Three in-process quota arguments share that naming convention and are declared as non-wire shapes with reasons; the suite then proves each exclusion honest by checking that it is genuinely absent from `ArcanumJsonContext`. An exception list living quietly inside the test would let the next one hide behind it.

**Contexts own disjoint sets.** API payloads resolve through `ArcanumJsonContext`, CLI-only `*Payload` values through `CliJsonContext`, and the two durable recovery checkpoints through the Infrastructure-owned `CovenantRecoveryJsonContext`, in the same way `BackupJsonContext` already works. The separation is not tidiness: a checkpoint is read by a process several releases newer than the one that wrote it, and a public wire context is governed by an entirely different compatibility promise. Sharing one would mean a wire change could rewrite recovery state. `CovenantRecoveryJsonContext` sets `UnmappedMemberHandling = Disallow` and its codec caps payloads at 4 KiB before parsing, so an unknown field, an unknown version, malformed bytes, and an oversized payload all resolve to one content-free `Covenant.ManualRecoveryRequired` — an unrecognized field is an invariant the writer was maintaining and this build is not, and resuming a database-replacing operation with one of its guarantees silently dropped is worse than refusing to resume.

**Every enum on the wire travels as a name.** Four domain enums became reachable from a public shape in this slice and gained `StringOnlyJsonStringEnumConverter`; the inventory suite walks every property of every declared shape and fails on any enum without it, and a companion test proves a numeric enum is actually rejected rather than merely undocumented. An integer on the wire ties the contract to a declaration order nobody promised to keep.

**A protected response owns its lease until the last byte.** `CovenantProtectedJsonResult<T>` and `CovenantProtectedStreamResult` take the caller's `ICovenantOperationLease`, revalidate it immediately *before* the first byte, seal the response `no-store` with any `ETag` and `Last-Modified` removed, serialize through the source-generated type info, and release the lease in a `finally`. The window they close is the one between "the store answered" and "the bytes reached the client": a handler that released its lease on return would still be serializing protected content while a reset was draining, and the operator would be told erasure was complete by the same process writing the erased content down a socket. Both are single-use — executing twice would write a second body under a lease already released — and the stream result disposes its source on every path, including refusal, because a stream over protected content is itself a handle erasure has to drain.

**A durable operation can be named by its caller.** `long_running_operation_request_identities` has shipped since the persistence slice with nothing writing to it. `ILongRunningOperationStore.ResolveOrCreateAsync` is that writer: the operation row and the identity row commit in one `Serializable` transaction, the same name with the same apply-request digest replays the operation it already created, and the same name with a different digest returns `Security.IdempotencyConflict` and creates nothing. `StartWithRequestIdentityAsync` leases only a newly created operation — a replayed one may still be owned and running, and handing a second owner the same database-replacing work is exactly what the identity exists to prevent. The apply-request digest is stable across restart, boot salt, timestamps, and key version, so replay survives all four; an operation whose only replay key is the HTTP response announcing it has no replay key once that process is gone.

**Three shipped codes were reaching operators as 500s.** `Covenant.CampaignBindingConflict` and `Session.CampaignBindingRequired` are produced by the canonical Campaign resolver and the assistant-begin path, and `Covenant.HostToolsTransitionRequired` by the startup gate; none had a mapper arm, so all three resolved through the default to **500**. They are now **409**, **409**, and **503**, alongside the full frozen table below. `Covenant.IneligibleTurn` deliberately has no HTTP arm: it is MCP-only, because the operator API arrives with authenticated authority and never has to ask whether the turn carried a staging capability.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| Shared bounded-request rules | `CovenantWireValidation` |
| Frozen management, mutation, and recovery shapes | `Covenant*Contracts.cs` in `Core/Covenant/` |
| Closed inventory of shapes, ports, and exclusions | `CovenantPublicContractInventory` |
| The five service seams #87 and #89 implement | `ICovenantManagementService` and its four peers |
| Lease-bound JSON and stream responses | `CovenantProtectedJsonResult<T>`, `CovenantProtectedStreamResult` |
| Durable recovery payloads and their context | `CovenantRecoveryCheckpointCodec` → `CovenantRecoveryJsonContext` |
| Caller-named durable operation identity | `ILongRunningOperationStore.ResolveOrCreateAsync` |
| Frozen status mapping | `ArcanumErrorMapper.ResolveStatusCode` |

**What is deliberately absent.** No route is mapped, no command is registered, no port has an implementation, and `Arcanum:Features:Covenant` still does not exist. Those arrive with #89 (operator, Campaign, and configuration surfaces) and #87 (maintenance and protected-erasure recovery), and the two new `LongRunningOperationKinds` for index rebuild and family reinitialize arrive with #87 beside the recovery handlers the registry requires them to have. Prompt bytes, token totals, and the disabled-path command count are unchanged, because nothing in this slice executes on any path.

### 10.17 Covenant maintenance and protected-erasure recovery

Issue #87 builds the two kernels every destructive Covenant path shares, then uses them for schema repair, index rebuild, and family reinitialize. Retention purge (#94), reset, and family reinitialize all delete the same artifacts for different reasons, and three independent switch statements over the same enum is exactly how one of them ends up preserving evidence the other two remove.

**One registry owns every database-artifact deletion policy.** `CovenantSensitiveArtifactPurgePolicy` is a literal, exhaustive table over all thirteen `SensitiveArtifactKind` values: which projections go, whether the Session's current pointer and sensitivity projection are repaired, whether an immutable erasure receipt replaces the deleted content, whether a durable terminal proof is required first, and which finalization, claim, and replay-denial evidence survives. `Resolve` returns a typed refusal for anything unmapped rather than a nullable, because the only correct response to an artifact a purge cannot classify is to stop — skipping it would report success while leaving Covenant-derived content in place. `external_disclosure_receipts`, the folded aggregates, and joined disclosure state are outside the table and cannot be selected through it: erasing the local copy of a byte a provider received does not un-receive it, and a purge that could quietly drop the evidence would turn an honest "nonrevocable" report into a clean one.

Where those rows currently live is a separate table. `CovenantArtifactPurgePlans` maps each kind to the statements this build's schema actually supports, so the policy can be exhaustive over thirteen kinds while the schema is not yet: nothing in this build creates a tool artifact, protected search projection, audit payload, notification, or turn-evidence row, so those kinds resolve to a label-only plan — the label, which is the only durable record that an artifact is Covenant-derived, is still removed, and there is no content row to find. When that storage lands, its owning slice adds statements to the plan table and the policy does not move.

**The authority is borrowed, never owned.** `CovenantArtifactErasureAuthority` is a closed, nonserializable two-arm union. `Ordinary` carries a live `CovenantWriteLease` plus an `OperatorAuthorityContext` issued for `SensitivityRetentionPurge`; `Exclusive` carries a live exclusive lease registered under `CovenantFamilyReinitialize`, `CovenantReset`, or `HealthyCatalogFactoryErasure` and rejects path mutation, Campaign delete, protected transfer, schema repair, and restore outright. It exposes revalidation and the lease's coverage and nothing else — no `CompleteAsync`, no `DisposeAsync`, no lease accessor — so a kernel handed one can never reopen admission on the caller's behalf, and an exclusive caller cannot self-deadlock by having a kernel acquire an ordinary lease during its own drain. Ordinary construction compares the authority epoch and nothing else, because that is the only fact the lease snapshot and the operator context genuinely share; aliasing another generation field to manufacture a second comparison would be a check that proves nothing.

**Coverage is derived from the rows, not the request.** Both kernels reread the artifact and its exact label inside the transaction that will delete them, derive the owner scope from those current rows, and require it to fall inside the lease's coverage. Installation coverage covers every owner; a Global-only lease covers Global owners alone, because a Campaign's rows are not "part of" the Global scope even though they belong to the same installation, and treating them as though they were is how a Global purge quietly deletes one Campaign's memory. A page computed against a dataset generation the lease did not capture is refused before the first statement.

**One managed-file kernel owns everything outside SQLite.** A deletion crosses a boundary the database cannot roll back, so exactly one implementation owns the sequence: reread the producer's `AdoptedAndLabeled` row at its exact revision, copy its complete `ManagedFileDurableLocationEvidence` and `ManagedFileOwnershipEvidence` into a `local_erasure_work_items` row, then open no-follow, verify from the same handle, compare-delete, fsync the retained parent, and complete. The request carries only identities — no path, root, revision, segment, parent identity, leaf, ownership value, or scope — because a caller-supplied location is precisely how a confused or hostile request aims a deleter at a file Arcanum never created. The opener revalidates the recorded root identity, traverses only the recorded normalized segments without following a link at any step, and compares the parent's identity from the same retained handle before the leaf is opened. Only a leaf that is genuinely absent at that exact resolved location records `AlreadyAbsent`; a changed root, revision, parent, identity, length, or content hash is a manual blocker that leaves the file, the producer row, and the label untouched.

The database enforces the ordering rather than the C#. `artifact_sensitivity_guard_delete` requires a purge or family-maintenance scope; `managed_file_write_intents_guard_update` refuses the writer's own scope on the adoption-to-erasure edge, requires *exactly one* erasure scope, requires a matching `DeletionVerified` work item, and requires the label to be gone already; `local_erasure_work_items_guard_update` refuses to complete until that producer row is `Erased`. Requiring exactly one erasure scope rather than at least one means a caller cannot hold both and let the trigger pick whichever explanation fits.

**Startup adopts what a crash left behind.** `CovenantLocalErasureStartupRecovery` is the sole pre-readiness adopter of nonterminal work items. It runs under the caller's already-held installation lock — asserted, never reacquired and never disposed — and it never reconstructs the lost write lease or operator context, because those are capabilities and a restart cannot honestly reissue one. The narrowly scoped restart authority is instead the pair both the database and the operating system can still prove: a durable work item the insert guard already authorized, plus the live installation lock. It drives the same state machine the live kernel uses, so a recovery pass cannot have its own opinion about which file is Arcanum's. A proven mismatch is `ManualEvidenceReady` and does not block unrelated startup; an unreadable row, a missing producer, or storage uncertainty is `Blocked`.

**Schema repair is journal-first and narrow.** Repair mutates the catalog, so recovery cannot work out who owned an interrupted operation by inspecting the result — a half-installed family looks the same whether this process created it or found it. `covenant_schema_repair_intents` is committed with the complete `CovenantExclusiveRecoveryOwner` *before* the first repair statement, and its identity fields, digests, action, tier, captured generation, and authority epoch are all immutable. The three permitted actions install a wholly absent canonical family, rerun the idempotent installer over an existing validated one, or recreate a missing ordinary index; nothing repairs a trigger, because writes may have occurred while its invariant was absent and restoring the trigger would restore the guarantee without restoring the data it protected. `CovenantSchemaRepairStartupRecovery` resumes only against the exact catalog digest the journal recorded, adopts the durable owner into the gate before readiness is published, and resumes the registration rather than acquiring a new one — an initial acquisition for an existing intent would give one closed scope two owners each believing it may reopen.

**Exactly one disposition, chosen from evidence.** `CovenantExclusiveDisposition.Select` is a pure function over four facts — storage verified, authority verified, durably mutated, health published — with one outcome per combination. Unverified storage or authority is `KeepClosed` however much else is proven. A proven no-mutation is `RollbackAndReopen`. A durable mutation whose transition is published is `CommitAndReopen`; a durable mutation without publication is `KeepClosed`. The asymmetry is the point: reopening after an unproven commit hands live traffic a database nobody verified, while staying closed costs availability and preserves the operation, because `KeepClosed` keeps the recovery owner. The lease claims its one disposition *before* the attempt, so a failed disposition can never be retried under a different code, and the one-shot journal finalizer runs only after a disposition actually succeeded — after a failed one the journal stays at `ReopenPending` on purpose, which makes the operation resumable rather than recording a terminal phase nobody proved.

**Two durable kinds, each with one of everything.** `covenant-index-rebuild` and `covenant-family-reinitialize` each have exactly one registry descriptor, one registered handler, one checkpoint version, and one transition owner. Index rebuild uses the server-generated identity arm only — it replaces a derived accelerator, so there is no authenticated preflight to name it after and nothing for a caller to bind an apply request to — and its coordinator owns only identity, checkpointing, and recovery scheduling, delegating each idempotent batch to Plan 02's single `CovenantIndexRebuilder`. Family reinitialize is the one Covenant kind carrying a caller-supplied durable identity, constructed only through `CovenantRequestedOperationStarter`; its gate owner always uses the durable server operation ID, never the caller's replay key, because two callers replaying the same name must resolve to the same operation and an owner built from the replay key would let the second adopt the first's closed scope. It erases every protected artifact and managed file through the shared kernels *before* anything is dropped, and a manual file blocker leaves the family intact and admission closed rather than destroying the database's own record of a file it cannot prove it removed.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| The exhaustive 13-kind deletion policy | `CovenantSensitiveArtifactPurgePolicy` |
| Where each kind's rows live in this build | `CovenantArtifactPurgePlans` |
| The borrowed two-arm erasure capability | `CovenantArtifactErasureAuthority` |
| Database-owned artifact deletion | `CovenantProtectedArtifactErasureKernel` |
| Everything outside SQLite | `CovenantManagedFileErasureKernel` + `ManagedFileErasureStateMachine` |
| Pre-readiness adoption of unfinished deletions | `CovenantLocalErasureStartupRecovery` |
| The schema-repair journal and its resume | `CovenantSchemaRepairJournal`, `CovenantSchemaRepairStartupRecovery` |
| The single reopening decision | `CovenantExclusiveDisposition` |
| Durable maintenance operation identity | `CovenantIndexRebuildCoordinator`, `CovenantFamilyReinitializeCoordinator` |

**What is deliberately absent.** No route is mapped and no command is registered: `CovenantMemoryEndpoints` and the maintenance CLI arrive with #89, and `ICovenantMaintenanceService.RepairSchemaAsync` and `RebuildIndexAsync` are implemented against the frozen port so that slice has something to call. The two reinitialize methods return the frozen `Covenant.ManualRecoveryRequired` code until #89 supplies the authenticated all-installation preflight and the token issuer a signed plan needs; returning an unsigned plan would hand a caller a token nothing can later verify. The public `LongRunningOperationPublicProgress` catalog the coordinated plan reserves codes 17 and 18 in is owned by an unshipped Plan 03 slice and is not invented here. Prompt bytes and token totals are unchanged, because nothing in this slice executes on a turn path.

### 10.18 Covenant operator surfaces, configuration, and the pre-binding authority boundary

Issue #89 is the first Covenant slice that an operator can reach. It lands the authenticated request boundary every later Covenant route is built on, the feature gate that turns the whole subsystem on, and the disclosure an operator reads before flipping it. **It is a partial delivery: the boundary, the configuration surface, and two backfilled Plan 03 layers are in; the typed Covenant routes, the Campaign-path and Session-binding administration surfaces, and the CLI are not.** The absent half is enumerated at the end of this section rather than left to be discovered.

**Order is the contract, and it is decided before the body is read.** The sequence is API-key authentication, then `X-Arcanum-Context-Policy` validation, then Covenant authority issuance, and only then body-size enforcement and source-generated binding. Minimal-API endpoint filters run *after* model binding, so a check that lived in a filter alone would already have had the body read, buffered, and deserialized — and `POST /v1/files` raises the multipart ceiling to 513 MiB, which makes "after binding" mean half a gigabyte spooled to the temp filesystem for a caller who was never going to be allowed to send it. Authentication stays strictly first: a wrong key presented alongside a malformed policy header is a **401**, not a 400, because a 400 would confirm to an unauthenticated caller both that they reached a real route and that their header spelling was the only thing wrong with the request.

**One legal value, and every other spelling is refused.** `X-Arcanum-Context-Policy` accepts exactly one lowercase ASCII `none`, or nothing at all. `NONE`, `None`, a leading or trailing space, an empty value, and `none,none` are all 400s, and two headers are refused rather than merged. The strictness is not pedantry: this header suppresses durable memory injection, so a caller that wrote `None` intending suppression and silently received `Default` would send content it believed it had excluded. A refusal is recoverable; a permissive parse is a disclosure. HTTP permits repetition, and every merge rule — first wins, last wins, comma-join — is a guess about which of two disagreeing senders meant it. A route that never injects Covenant content rejects the header outright rather than ignoring it, for the same reason, and the policy the server actually applied is echoed back and exposed through CORS, because a client that can send the header but never read the decision is guessing.

**The decision is recorded once and cannot be revised.** `CovenantRequestFeatures` writes the context policy and the typed authority into the request exactly once. A downstream filter that could relax the policy would turn "the operator asked for no durable context" from a decision into a suggestion, and one that could swap the authority context would let a route accept authority issued for a different requirement. `CovenantAuthorityEndpointFilter` remains as defence in depth: it rechecks — never reissues — the epoch after binding, because a reissue would silently adopt whatever generation is now current, which is exactly the mid-flight host-tools taint or key rotation the recheck exists to catch.

**The protected header tuple is exact, and it is registered on response start.** Every protected success and failure emits `Cache-Control: no-store, private`, `Pragma: no-cache`, and `Expires: 0`, with any `ETag` and `Last-Modified` removed. Each element earns its place: `no-store` is the modern instruction, `private` survives the intermediaries that treat an unqualified `no-store` as advisory, and `Pragma`/`Expires` are for the HTTP/1.0-era caches that ignore `Cache-Control` entirely. A validator is removed rather than merely not set, because a validator on a no-store response is the combination a permissive intermediary resolves in the wrong direction, and a 304 is a cache hit on content the installation may since have erased. It is applied through `HttpResponse.OnStarting` rather than by each result type, because "every protected success or failure" has to include the framework-generated 400 and the plain `Results.Ok` nobody wrote a result type for.

**Streaming may set the default and may never weaken it.** `InferenceExecuteWriter` and `SseStreamWriter` no longer assign `Cache-Control: no-cache` directly; they call a helper that applies the protected tuple when the request is protected and the shipped streaming default otherwise. The asymmetry is one-way for a physical reason: headers cannot be corrected once the first byte has left, so a writer that assigned the weaker value unconditionally would downgrade a protected stream undetectably. The reverse matters too — an explicit-`none`, untainted response that is genuinely eligible for the generic response cache keeps the shipped default, because stamping every stream private would quietly delete a shipped optimization in the name of a protection it does not need.

**Ports borrow leases; they never acquire them.** The five ports #88 froze now take the caller-owned `ICovenantSnapshotReadLease` or `CovenantWriteLease`, matching `ICovenantStore` beneath them. The endpoint acquires, the service borrows, and the response holds it through serialization. A service that could escalate its own coverage would make the gate's drain guarantee unprovable, because a reader could appear inside an operation that had already finished draining. `ExplainAsync` is the deliberate exception and detaches its own state lease through `CovenantLeasedServiceResult<T>`: it evaluates a *fresh* management snapshot with the live loader, linker, and admission code, so borrowing the caller's lease would either explain a different snapshot than the one it ran or force a nested acquisition — and a nested acquisition inside a drain is a deadlock. `CampaignPathIdentityService` gained a fourth method for the same reason a replay must be cheap: `ProbeApplyAsync` answers "you already did this" under a short read lease, because closing and draining a Campaign's admission to answer the most common request this surface sees would make the cheapest request the most disruptive one.

**A live transfer, not a released one.** `CovenantLeasedServiceResult<T>` and its exclusive variant move a live lease from the service that read under it to the response that will release it. Releasing on return is the one thing a protected read must not do: the handler would hold no coverage while the body was being written, and a reset draining in that window would report erasure complete while the same process finished writing the erased bytes down a socket. Both are single-take — two takers means two disposals of one registration, and a delayed second release can free a gate slot a later lease already owns — and both carry a lease on the failure path, because the refusal travels through the same boundary and a boundary holding coverage only on success would have none exactly while it wrote the body explaining the content is gone. The exclusive variant carries the disposition rather than letting the boundary choose it: the boundary knows only whether serialization threw, while whether the operation durably mutated anything, and whether that mutation was published, are facts only the coordinator observed.

**One copy for every irreversible disclosure.** `CovenantExternalRetentionDisclosure` is the single source of the enablement and destructive-operation text. The Compendium toggle, the reinitialize prompt, the reset prompt, and protected restore all have to tell an operator the same true thing, and four surfaces each writing their own sentence is four subtly different promises — worse, a paraphrase drifts in one direction, because "Arcanum deletes your data" is easier to write than "Arcanum cannot un-send what a provider already received." Help targets are selected by **exact endpoint host or exact Familiar kind, never by a provider's display name**: a name is operator-authored text in `arcanum.json`, so a name-driven resolver would let a one-line edit point an operator at `openai.com` to read the retention policy of a gateway OpenAI has never seen a byte from — a wrong answer delivered with the full authority of an official link. Host matching is exact and case-insensitive, so `api.openai.com.evil.example` and `openai.com` are both unrecognized; a suffix match is how a lookalike host earns somebody else's documentation. Unknown, proxy, and self-hosted providers get the Compendium Providers page, and the operator-guide target is always appended.

**The feature is off, and the default is the guarantee.** `Arcanum:Features:Covenant` defaults `false`; an installation that never names the key adds no Covenant prompt bytes, performs no Covenant read, and produces byte-identical prompts to a build without the feature. It uses `{ get; set; }` rather than `init` because the configuration binding generator silently skips `init`-only properties (dotnet/runtime#107856), which would have left the feature permanently off while `arcanum.json` still said otherwise. `CovenantFeatureConfigurationPublisher` is the only bridge from that key to the in-memory availability gate and it touches no storage: a disable that had to reach SQLCipher to take effect would be unavailable exactly on a locked or degraded database, which is the wrong direction for the one control that must always work. Its callbacks are serialized and each republishes the monitor's *current* value rather than the one it was handed, because `IOptionsMonitor` promises no ordering and two rapid edits delivered out of order would otherwise leave an installation enabled after the operator's last action was to disable it. A disable becomes visible at the next in-memory gate read; a turn already in flight aborts at its required pre-dispatch revalidation, because a provider call that has already left the process cannot be recalled by a configuration edit.

**Disclosure before decision, in construction order.** For the `features.covenant` descriptor the generic settings editor renders the full shared disclosure and every resolved help action *before* it constructs the toggle, and the test asserts construction order rather than the presence of the pieces. A warning rendered underneath the switch is read after the decision, which makes it a receipt rather than a warning. Only a `ProviderRetentionDocumentation` target is handed to the Avalonia URI launcher; the other two arms are an in-app page and a repository document, and treating all three as links is how an internal route reaches a browser.

**Backfilled because Plan 03 shipped it in reduced form.** `session_turn_claims` and `session_turn_maintenance_steps` have been installed since #81 with **no C# reader or writer**, and `SessionTurnClaimState` and `CovenantQuotaGuard.ReserveClaimAndFinalizationAsync` had no callers. `SessionTurnClaimStore` is that writer. It keys on the schema's own `(OriginInstallationId, ClientTurnId)` uniqueness rather than the `(session, key)` pair the index does not actually enforce, and treats a replay naming a different Session as `Security.IdempotencyConflict`; the one-live-claim-per-Session half comes from the separate partial index and surfaces as `Hub.SessionTurnBusy`. The future assistant Entry ID lives on `assistant_finalization_capacity_reservations`, not on the claim, because the claim's own `CHECK` keeps `AssistantEntryId` NULL until begin — so the store reads it back rather than minting a second one.

**Memory status reports capability, never content.** The Covenant block on `/api/memory/status` carries counts, ceilings, and closed health codes only, because that route is reachable without Covenant read authority; the exact per-entry surfaces are the typed Covenant routes, which require authority of their own. A `null` block means the tier is absent from this composition, which is deliberately distinct from present-but-unavailable — a block reporting zeros for both would tell an operator their Covenant is empty when in fact it could not be read.

| Concern | Owner |
|---|---|
| The one legal context-policy wire value | `CovenantContextPolicyParser` |
| Write-once per-request policy and authority | `CovenantRequestFeatures` |
| The exact protected header tuple | `CovenantProtectedResponseHeaders` |
| Route authority declaration and epoch recheck | `CovenantEndpointConventionBuilderExtensions`, `CovenantAuthorityEndpointFilter` |
| Live lease hand-off from service to response | `CovenantLeasedServiceResult<T>`, `CovenantExclusiveLeasedServiceResult<T>` |
| The one disclosure copy and its help targets | `CovenantExternalRetentionDisclosure` |
| Configuration to in-memory gate | `CovenantFeatureConfigurationPublisher` |
| Durable Session turn claims | `SessionTurnClaimStore` (`ISessionTurnClaimCoordinator`) |

**What is deliberately absent, and still owed by #89.** No Covenant route is mapped yet, so `CovenantMemoryEndpoints`, the cursor protector, the operator mutation service, and the maintenance doctor routes remain unshipped, and `ICovenantMaintenanceService` is still unregistered dead code with its two family-reinitialize methods returning `Covenant.ManualRecoveryRequired`. The Campaign-path and Session-binding administration services, their stores, and their startup recovery are unbuilt, and `CampaignPathApplyProbe` currently has no producer. No `arcanum memory covenant`, `campaign path`, `session campaign-binding`, or `security host-process-tools` command is registered. `InferenceIdempotencyPolicy` does not exist, so Session-backed inference still uses the generic response-cache path and the cache fingerprint does not yet include the context policy. The conditional derived-read guard, its closed endpoint inventory, the `ForkSession` protected-transfer rewrite, and labeled live-stream events all still need the Plan 03 ports (`IProtectedDerivedReadStore`, `LabeledSessionEntryEvent`, `CovenantPreviewServiceResult<T>`) that issues #83–#86 specified and did not ship. `IProtectedArtifactTransferStore` and `ICampaignPathMarkerLifecycle` were the two of that set issue #90 had to build before it could proceed; see [§10.19.5](#10195-selective-transfer-and-the-two-ports-this-slice-had-to-build-first) for what they cover, [§10.19.7](#10197-proving-a-reopened-campaign-root-after-a-restart) for the restart arm issue #110 added to the marker lifecycle, [§10.19.8](#10198-recovering-restore-topology-and-authority-before-readiness) for the startup recovery issue #111 wired them into, and [§10.19.9](#10199-what-is-deliberately-absent-and-still-owed-by-90) for what they still do not. Prompt bytes and token totals are unchanged, because nothing in this slice executes on a turn path.

### 10.19 Covenant backup, restore, and protected transfer

Issue #90. A physical backup of a Covenant-enabled installation is a **nonrevocable disclosure**: once the archive exists, deleting the installation does not unmake it. Restore is the mirror image — it is the one operation that can move protected state, authority, and filesystem capabilities across a machine boundary. This section covers what that slice built for both, §10.19.6 covers the authenticated journal issue #109 added on top of it, §10.19.7 covers the reopened-root proof issue #110 added, §10.19.8 covers the two-phase startup recovery issue #111 added, and §10.19.9 records exactly what is still owed.

#### 10.19.1 Two barriers before any physical backup byte

`CovenantBackupDisclosureBoundary` commits a durable receipt **before** the snapshot reads page one, and again **before** the archive codec can write its first byte. Both go through `ICovenantDisclosureJournal` under `CovenantDisclosureEffectCategory.EncryptedBackup`, and a commit failure prevents the corresponding effect rather than being reported after it. A journal written afterwards cannot record the one case it exists for — a backup that produced bytes and then crashed — and the operator would be told nothing left while an archive sat on disk.

The two effects are counted separately. Reading the protected database and writing an encrypted archive fail independently and leave different residue, so a single backup-wide receipt would report a disclosure that did not happen or miss one that did. Each retry allocates a **new physical attempt ordinal**, so a second attempt at the same effect is a second physical disclosure rather than a replay of the first: counting is allowed to overstate and never to understate.

Neither receipt names where the archive went. `DestinationIdentity` is a digest over the resolved output path under `Arcanum.Covenant.BackupDestinationIdentity.v1`; storing the destination itself would recreate inside the journal the exposure the journal exists to account for. `BackupOperationCheckpoint` gained the disclosure subject and both acknowledged receipt digests and ordinals, so crash recovery closes or resumes the same subject instead of opening a second one.

**One installation read lease, retained across both effects.** `BackupService.CreateAsync` acquires `AcquireInstallationReadAsync` before the inventory is frozen and disposes it only after the archive writer has completed or failed — never between the snapshot and the last byte. No backup layer acquires a nested Global or Campaign lease; the test gate throws on every scoped acquisition, so a nested lease anywhere under the backup fails the run rather than passing quietly. Without that retention, reset could drain one scope while another was still being copied into the archive.

The whole path is behind `Arcanum:Features:Covenant`. With the gate off, `CovenantBackupServices` is null, no receipt is written, no lease is taken, and a full backup is byte-for-byte what it was before this slice.

#### 10.19.2 The restore effect digest

`BackupRestoreEffectDigestCalculator` is the sole producer of the identity that binds a restore's exclusive owner to one exact destructive plan. The same digest is what the gate acquisition, the recovery journal, each marker child row, and every retry's adoption check compare against, so two implementations that agreed on the day they were written would, at their first divergence, let a changed plan resume a half-finished destructive sequence.

The preimage is domain `Arcanum.Covenant.BackupRestore.Effect.v1`, then the archive-manifest digest, archive physical-identity digest, profile-namespace digest, RFC-4122 installation UUID bytes, destination-root identity digest, the one-byte conflict code, a length-prefixed ASCII protected-state **wire value**, the path-mapping vector digest, and the `RestoreMasterApiKey` and `CreateSafetyBackup` bytes. The wire value rather than the numeric code, so reordering the enum later cannot silently turn one operator's *purge* into another's *restore* while producing the same digest.

Dry-run and confirmation flags are excluded because they authorize no effect — including them would make a rehearsal and the real restore it rehearsed produce different owners, so the real run could not adopt the plan the operator just reviewed. `ImportSelectedSessions` is refused outright: selective import authorizes no installation-level effect and carries its own transfer digest.

`DestinationRootIdentity` binds the no-follow parent identity, the bounded child leaf, and **whether the root is present** — never path text. "The root exists" and "the root is absent" have to be different destinations, or a `NewProfileRoot` owner could be replayed over a root that meanwhile came into existence. `PathMappingVector` orders by kind, then source, then destination with a checked count, so a reordered command line is the same plan and produces the same owner.

#### 10.19.3 Destination-monotonic joins

`CovenantAuthorityStateJoiner` merges archived authority into the destination in exactly one direction. Host-tools taint is ordered `Clean < PendingHostToolsTaint < HostToolsTainted` and the join takes the maximum, so an archive taken on a clean machine **cannot** clear this machine's taint: the escape happened here, and a file copied in from a machine where it did not happen is not evidence that it did not happen here. The reverse holds too — restoring content that was exposed elsewhere brings that exposure with it.

Source authority is never imported. The archive's installation identity, epoch, and transition belong to a different installation, and adopting them would let a restore claim a lineage this machine never had. The restored row carries the destination's own identity and an epoch one past **both** lineages, because every read authority issued under either has to stop being valid.

`CovenantDisclosureStateJoiner` owns reads and writes only and delegates every value decision to `CovenantDisclosureStateAlgebra.JoinRestore`. The join has to be a semilattice — commutative, associative, idempotent — and a second implementation living beside the storage would drift from the one the rest of the system reasons about. A joined bucket becomes a `LowerBound`: two installations' counts cannot be added without double-counting the effects they share, and taking the smaller would understate what left.

#### 10.19.4 Stripping managed-file authority out of staging

Restore never inherits managed-file authority. A backup taken on one machine describes files that do not exist on this one, so every restored `managed_file_write_intents` row and every `local_erasure_work_items` row is a delete capability for a path this installation never owned. Removing them is not cleanup — it is the removal of authority, which is why it is the one operation `ICovenantSqliteConnectionInitializer.Authorize` refuses outright.

`RestoreStagingManagedAuthoritySanitizationCapability` is sealed, nonserializable, single-take, and mintable only from a complete `BackupRestore` owner over a candidate connection that has **never been published as live**. It exports no connection, transaction, command, delegate, callback, or generic execution method. Its only caller-facing operation is `RunImmediateAsync`, which takes the capability exactly once with `Interlocked.CompareExchange`, begins the immediate transaction, mints a nested `RunIdentity`, and invokes only the compile-time-bound static `BackupRestoreManagedAuthoritySanitizer.ExecuteInSessionAsync`.

The grant is producer-only. `AuthorizeRestoreStagingManagedAuthoritySanitization` accepts no raw connection and no general authorization kind; it calls the capability's `BorrowCode11Scope`, which requires reference equality with both the retained initializer and the **active run identity**. The identity is minted inside the one invocation and invalidated before control returns, so a scope obtained during that invocation cannot be replayed by a later one.

`RestoreStagingManagedAuthoritySanitizationSession` has exactly eight typed operations and a monotonic private ordinal:

| Order | Operation | Why here |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `InventoryManagedWriteIntentsAsync` | Every arm — pending, adopted, erased, completed, manual |
| 2 | `InsertAndValidateManagedSourceTombstonesAsync` | Source tombstones exist before anything links to them |
| 3 | `InventoryLocalErasureWorkItemsAsync` | — |
| 4 | `InsertAndValidateLinkedLocalTombstonesAsync` | Copies artifact, label, owner scope, and disposition **from the source tombstone** |
| 5 | `GuardDeleteLocalErasureWorkItemsAsync` | Row by row, so a row that appeared after the inventory survives |
| 6 | `DeleteExactAdoptedLabelsAsync` | After the rows that referenced them |
| 7 | `GuardDeleteManagedWriteIntentsAsync` | — |
| 8 | `VerifyCompleteAsync` | Both authority tables empty, one tombstone per enumerated row |

Step 4 copying from step 2's row rather than from its own is the schema's rule, not a convenience: a local row that read its own disposition could invent one its producer never recorded, and `restored_managed_file_authority_tombstones_guard_insert` refuses exactly that. A skipped, repeated, reordered, escaped, or foreign session fails **before** it reaches SQL, and any failure rolls back and changes no counter, so a rejected check can never be mistaken for a completed sanitation.

`TombstoneVectorDigest` commits to everything the run stripped under `Arcanum.BackupRestore.ManagedAuthorityTombstones.v1`, sorted by source-kind code then source-row identity so two runs over the same staged database agree regardless of the order SQLite returned rows in. Zero source rows use the same preimage with a checked zero and no item bytes. The sanitizer resolves no managed-file opener, no ownership verifier, and no workspace root, and makes **no filesystem call at all**: the rows it removes describe files on a different machine, and touching them would be acting on authority this installation has just proven it does not have.

#### 10.19.5 Selective transfer, and the two ports this slice had to build first

Two ports that issues #83–#89 specified and did not ship were prerequisites, so this slice built them.

**`ICampaignPathMarkerLifecycle`** (Plan 04 Task 7, owed by #89). `PrepareRestoreCleanupInStagedDatabaseAsync` commits one `RestoreCleanup` child per Campaign whose marker the restore must remove, inside the caller's **staged** core transaction — staged rather than live because replacement erases the live database, so a cleanup intent written there would be destroyed by the very swap it exists to survive. It borrows the connection and transaction without beginning, committing, rolling back, or disposing either.

Preparation observes each marker through an already-opened root and commits a one-way digest of the exact bytes it saw; it never stores the marker payload, because a cleanup that kept it would be holding a per-registration secret in order to delete a file. A Campaign whose marker is **proven absent** contributes no child at all: a durable row whose only outcome is "there was nothing to do" would need a sentinel digest standing in for "no bytes", and a sentinel is one substitution away from matching a real marker. A marker naming a different Campaign or revision blocks the whole preparation rather than being removed.

`CampaignPathRestoreCleanupIntentVector` is the canonical commitment. The zero arm is why it exists as a frozen literal — `4da7d983be83d8a8f9c50e927319a6297f451d9c7366ce0df0506882fd1fee64`, the SHA-256 of the domain, one `0x00`, and a checked `UInt64BE` zero.
